Mosca's inequality (x + y > z): x = 5, y = 3, z = 12 → time available under this scenario. z is a planning assumption chosen per risk profile, not a prediction. A cryptographically relevant quantum computer is not publicly known to exist today.

Post-quantum maturity: Level 0 of 4 — no post-quantum cryptography. Next step: Inventory where classical public-key cryptography protects long-lived data and keys, and choose the first migration: usually a hybrid TLS key exchange and wrapped keys.

Methodology

The audit was performed by the QSI engine 1.2.0. Each analyzer emits facts (rule, parameters, evidence); finding texts are generated from the rule catalog. Methodology sources are listed under "Sources and normative documents".

Prioritization uses an explainable weighted model (severity, exploitability, exposure, confidentiality/integrity/availability impact weighted by the "Small technology company" profile, asset criticality, urgency, ease of remediation) with a confidence multiplier. CVSS v3.1 is applied only to technical vulnerabilities with a defined vector. Long-term quantum risks use Mosca's inequality; their severity is capped at Medium and they are never presented as exploitable vulnerabilities.

Finding classes: confirmed technical vulnerability; known vulnerable component (a dependency version named in an advisory; whether the affected code is reachable is assessed separately, and a component confirmed by review becomes a vulnerability); confirmed configuration error; potential cryptographic risk; long-term post-quantum risk; improvement recommendation; insufficient data. Heuristic findings are marked preliminary; analyst decisions are shown next to the findings.

Detailed findings

QSI-2026-04273 · DEP-KNOWN-ADVISORYHigh

openssl 0.10.64 is affected by GHSA-8c75-8mhr-p7r9 (CVE-2026-41678)

Known vulnerable componentConfidence: Medium

OSV.dev reports GHSA-8c75-8mhr-p7r9 (CVE-2026-41678) for crates.io package openssl at version 0.10.64: rust-openssl has incorrect bounds assertion in aes key wrap. The advisory names no affected functions, so reachability was not determined.

Affected asset
dependency:crates.io/openssl@0.10.64
Data source
OSV.dev
Detection method
OSV.dev querybatch API lookup of the exact declared version.
Analyzer
dependencies 1.2.0 · 2026-10-28 12:15 UTC
Priority score
51.3 / 100 (Small technology company)
CVSS v3.1
not applicable

Potential impact

Depends on whether the vulnerable code path is reachable in your application.

Reproduction steps

curl -s https://api.osv.dev/v1/vulns/GHSA-8c75-8mhr-p7r9

Evidence

tool_output · OSV.dev GHSA-8c75-8mhr-p7r9 · manifest · SHA-256 3aad5bd9d1c003ab…
{"id": "GHSA-8c75-8mhr-p7r9", "aliases": ["CVE-2026-41678"], "modified": "2026-09-10T03:50:43.807790048Z", "summary": "rust-openssl has incorrect bounds assertion in aes key wrap", "severity": [{"type": "CVSS_V4", "score": "CVSS:4.0/AV:N/AC:L/AT:P/PR:N/UI:N/VC:H/VI:H/VA:H/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N/E:U"}], "declared_in_manifest": true}

Limitations of the check

Advisory data comes from OSV.dev at the recorded time. Reachability is judged from names, not a call graph: the affected functions the advisory lists against the identifiers in the audited product code, a Cargo feature it says is off by default against the audited manifests, the platforms it lists against the declared Rust targets. Indirect calls (traits, macros, other dependencies) are not followed.

Recommendation

Upgrade to a fixed version (0.10.78).

QSI-2026-04238 · CHAINMAP-QVLow

Transaction signing for 1 chain(s) relies on elliptic-curve signatures

Long-term post-quantum riskConfidence: High

The wallet's message definitions cover Solana. These chains authorize transactions with Ed25519, which Shor's algorithm breaks on a cryptographically relevant quantum computer. A wallet cannot move a chain to post-quantum signatures on its own: the chain has to adopt a scheme first.

Affected asset
chain-map
Data source
storage-proto/proto/confirmed_block.proto
Detection method
Message definitions (.proto) matched to QSI's table of chain signature schemes.
Analyzer
chain_map 1.2.0 · 2026-10-28 12:15 UTC
Priority score
39.1 / 100 (Small technology company)
CVSS v3.1
not applicable
Mosca's inequality (x + y > z)
x=5.0, y=3.0, z=12.0 → time available under this scenario

Potential impact

Once a CRQC exists, an attacker who sees an account's public key could derive the private key and move funds; how soon keys are exposed depends on each chain (address reuse, account model).

Reproduction steps

Open the .proto files named in the evidence.

Evidence

file_excerpt · storage-proto/proto/confirmed_block.proto · line 3 · SHA-256 8e2b99d91a450afa…
package solana.storage.ConfirmedBlock;
file_excerpt · storage-proto/proto/entries.proto · line 3 · SHA-256 5d19891f93f3ce3e…
package solana.storage.Entries;
file_excerpt · storage-proto/proto/transaction_by_addr.proto · line 3 · SHA-256 1284fb928ee86825…
package solana.storage.TransactionByAddr;
file_excerpt · wen-restart/proto/wen_restart.proto · line 2 · SHA-256 663c888ac6e80064…
package solana.wen_restart_proto;

Limitations of the check

This is a forward-looking risk that depends on the arrival of a cryptographically relevant quantum computer (CRQC). No such computer is publicly known to exist today; the timeline is uncertain. It is not a present-day exploitable vulnerability. Chains are recognized from the names of the message definitions (.proto files and packages); the schemes are the chains' documented account signatures, not an analysis of the wallet's signing code.

Recommendation

Follow each chain's post-quantum roadmap, avoid public-key reuse, and keep signers ready to add post-quantum signature schemes as chains adopt them. Keep the signing layer crypto-agile per chain (algorithm identifiers in messages and storage, room for larger keys and signatures) and track each chain's post-quantum proposals, so that a new scheme can be added when a chain activates it.

Retest procedure

Re-run when a chain activates a post-quantum signature scheme or the wallet adds one.

P. W. Shor — Polynomial-Time Algorithms for Prime Factorization and Discrete Logarithms on a Quantum Computer (SIAM J. Comput., 1997); NIST IR 8547 — Transition to Post-Quantum Cryptography Standards

QSI-2026-04230 · TLSCODE-NO-PQ-HYBRIDLow

TLS without a post-quantum key exchange: rustls before 0.23 (ring only)

Long-term post-quantum riskConfidence: Mediumpreliminary — requires further verification

rustls before 0.23 (ring only) at Cargo.toml:289: the configured key exchange (ring) has no hybrid ML-KEM group such as X25519MLKEM768 (RFC 10024), so the handshake cannot negotiate one.

Affected asset
tls-code:rustls-old
Data source
Cargo.toml
Detection method
Static reading of TLS library configuration in code and manifests: crypto provider, group and curve lists, GODEBUG.
Analyzer
tls_code 1.2.0 · 2026-10-28 12:15 UTC
Priority score
28.2 / 100 (Small technology company)
CVSS v3.1
not applicable
Mosca's inequality (x + y > z)
x=5.0, y=3.0, z=12.0 → time available under this scenario

Potential impact

Traffic protected only by classical ECDHE could be recorded today and decrypted once a CRQC exists (harvest now, decrypt later). Relevant when transmitted data must stay confidential for many years.

Reproduction steps

Open Cargo.toml:289 and inspect the quoted lines; the evidence lists further files.

Evidence

file_excerpt · Cargo.toml · line 289 · SHA-256 1c1c37735d8f9da6… · File 8c4c8376ebed19b2…
  288 | rustc_version = "0.4"
  289 | rustls = { version = "0.21.10", default-features = false, features = ["quic"] }
  290 | rustversion = "1.0.14"

Limitations of the check

This is a forward-looking risk that depends on the arrival of a cryptographically relevant quantum computer (CRQC). No such computer is publicly known to exist today; the timeline is uncertain. It is not a present-day exploitable vulnerability. Read from code and manifests only: the library versions deployed and settings made outside the code (environment, launch flags) were not determined.

Recommendation

Upgrade rustls to 0.23 or later with the aws-lc-rs provider and the prefer-post-quantum feature.

Retest procedure

Connect with a client that offers X25519MLKEM768 (openssl s_client -groups X25519MLKEM768 with OpenSSL 3.5+, or a Go 1.24+ client) and confirm the negotiated group.

RFC 10024 — Hybrid ECDHE-MLKEM key agreement for TLS 1.3 (X25519MLKEM768, SecP256r1MLKEM768, SecP384r1MLKEM1024); IANA — Transport Layer Security (TLS) Parameters (Supported Groups, SignatureScheme, Cipher Suites); NIST IR 8547 — Transition to Post-Quantum Cryptography Standards; M. Mosca — Cybersecurity in an Era with Quantum Computers: Will We Be Ready? (IEEE Security & Privacy, 2018)

Post-quantum assessment

Key establishment (x = how long data must stay confidential; harvest now, decrypt later)Signatures (x = how long signatures/keys must stay trusted)
Data/key lifetime x55
Migration time y33
Assumed years to CRQC z12 (central)12
x + y − z-4 → time available under this scenario-4 → time available under this scenario

No signature lifetime is set for the project; the data lifetime is used as an assumption.

Planning assumptions: x for key establishment, x for signatures, y are the defaults of the risk profile “Small technology company”, not the client's figures; with the client's actual lifetimes the verdict can change.

Post-quantum maturity: Level 0 of 4 — no post-quantum cryptography

  • Classical only — channel key exchange (TLS, Noise, handshakes): TLS (classical groups) (1 file(s))

Next step: Inventory where classical public-key cryptography protects long-lived data and keys, and choose the first migration: usually a hybrid TLS key exchange and wrapped keys.

Levels: 0 none; 1 present, not wired; 2 in use outside the critical paths; 3 hybrid in a critical path; 4 post-quantum by default in the critical paths (root of trust, key wrapping, channel key exchange). Judged from code and manifests.

What the quantum-vulnerable algorithms do

RoleAlgorithms (files)IDFirst step
channel key exchange (TLS, Noise, handshakes)TLS ECDHE (1)QSI-2026-04230Move the handshake to a hybrid key exchange (X25519MLKEM768 in TLS 1.3; a hybrid KEM in Noise-based protocols)
threshold signing and MPC protocolsPedersen commitment (25)QSI-2026-04236Track post-quantum threshold signatures (NIST IR 8214C) and keep the protocol layer ready to swap the signature scheme; record which keys and shares must stay valid for how long
transaction and message signingEd25519 (5), ECDSA (3), BN254 (alt_bn128) (1)QSI-2026-04233, QSI-2026-04234, QSI-2026-04235, QSI-2026-04238Follow each chain's post-quantum roadmap, avoid public-key reuse, and keep signers ready to add post-quantum signature schemes as chains adopt them
role not determinedBN254 (alt_bn128) (10), Ed25519 (9), Bulletproofs (2)QSI-2026-04231, QSI-2026-04232, QSI-2026-04237Classify each usage, record data lifetimes, and plan migration to ML-DSA (FIPS 204) or SLH-DSA (FIPS 205), in a hybrid/composite form where the ecosystem supports it; introduce an abstraction layer for crypto-agility

Roles are judged from file paths, function names and the code around each match; review confirms them. Root-of-trust keys and wrapped keys come first: they are hard to replace or can be recorded today.

z is a planning assumption chosen per risk profile, not a prediction. A cryptographically relevant quantum computer is not publicly known to exist today.

Detailed findings: QSI-2026-04238, QSI-2026-04230, QSI-2026-04233, QSI-2026-04234, QSI-2026-04235, QSI-2026-04236, QSI-2026-04231, QSI-2026-04232, QSI-2026-04237

PQC readiness questionnaire

No readiness questionnaire was completed for this project.

Quantum algorithms relevant to this inventory

Quantum algorithmEffectInventoried algorithms (rows)What it means for this audit
Shor's algorithm — factoring
Q1: quantum computation offline, classical access to the victim		breaks RSA (29)Breaks RSA at every key length: a longer modulus only raises the cost polynomially. Plan migration to ML-KEM (or a hybrid) for key establishment and ML-DSA / SLH-DSA for signatures; start with keys protecting data that must stay confidential for years (harvest now, decrypt later) and with long-lived signatures.
Shor's algorithm — discrete logarithms in finite fields
Q1: quantum computation offline, classical access to the victim		breaks BN254 (alt_bn128) (15), ElGamal (4)Breaks finite-field Diffie–Hellman and DSA at every size. Move key establishment to ML-KEM (hybrid with X25519 or ECDH during the transition) and signatures to ML-DSA or SLH-DSA.
Shor's algorithm — elliptic-curve discrete logarithms
Q1: quantum computation offline, classical access to the victim		breaks BN254 (alt_bn128) (15), Bulletproofs (2), ECDSA (13), Ed25519 (15), Pedersen commitment (25)Elliptic-curve keys are expected to fall before RSA keys of comparable classical strength. On blockchains, coins whose public key is already on chain (reused addresses, pay-to-public-key and Taproot outputs) are exposed first; plan the move to post-quantum signatures and avoid key reuse in the meantime.
Grover's algorithm — key search and preimages
Q1: quantum computation offline, classical access to the victim		weakens (larger keys restore the margin) BLAKE3 (5), HMAC (1), Keccak-256 (3), SHA-1 (15), SHA-2 (27), SHA-3 (3), SHAKE (1)Not a break. 256-bit keys (AES-256, ChaCha20) keep a comfortable margin; AES-128 meets NIST category 1, while some authorities ask for 256-bit keys in quantum-resistant contexts (see the AES-128 entry). Prefer 256-bit keys for long-lived data; 256-bit hashes need no change for preimage resistance.
Brassard–Høyer–Tapp — collision search
Q1: quantum computation offline, classical access to the victim		weakens (larger keys restore the margin) BLAKE3 (5), Keccak-256 (3), SHA-1 (15), SHA-2 (27), SHA-3 (3), SHAKE (1)256-bit hashes (SHA-256, SHA3-256) remain adequate for collision resistance; 160-bit hashes are already weak classically (SHA-1 collisions are practical). Use 384/512-bit hashes where NIST categories 4–5 are required.

Effects: breaks — replace the algorithm; weakens — larger keys or outputs restore the margin; included in standard parameters — no action at standard parameter sets; only with superposition queries — not applicable to ordinary deployments.

Limitations and assumptions

  • This report presents automated and documented analysis results for the stated scope. It is not a legal opinion, a certification or an attestation of compliance with any standard.
  • z is a planning assumption chosen per risk profile, not a prediction. A cryptographically relevant quantum computer is not publicly known to exist today.

Sources and normative documents

Reviewed: 2026-10-13

  1. A. Chailloux, M. Naya-Plasencia, A. Schrottenloher — An Efficient Quantum Collision Search Algorithm and Implications on Symmetric Cryptography (ASIACRYPT 2017; IACR ePrint 2017/847) — IACR ePrint. https://eprint.iacr.org/2017/847 (peer-reviewed article: Õ(2^{2n/5}) time, O(n) qubits, Õ(2^{n/5}) classical memory)
  2. C. Chevignard, P.-A. Fouque, A. Schrottenloher — Reducing the Number of Qubits in Quantum Discrete Logarithms on Elliptic Curves (IACR ePrint 2026/280) — IACR ePrint. https://eprint.iacr.org/2026/280 (revision of a EUROCRYPT 2026 paper: 3.12n + o(n) qubits; for n = 256, 1193 qubits and 22 runs of 2^38.98 Toffoli gates)
  3. C. Gidney — How to factor 2048 bit RSA integers with less than a million noisy qubits (arXiv:2505.15917) — arXiv. https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.15917 (preprint (2025-05-21): < 1 million noisy qubits, < 1 week, 0.1% gate error, 1 µs surface-code cycle)
  4. C. Gidney, M. Ekerå — How to factor 2048 bit RSA integers in 8 hours using 20 million noisy qubits (Quantum 5, 433) — Quantum. https://doi.org/10.22331/q-2021-04-15-433 (peer-reviewed article (2021): 10^-3 gate error, 1 µs cycle, 10 µs reaction time)
  5. C. Zalka — Grover's quantum searching algorithm is optimal (Phys. Rev. A 60, 2746, 1999; arXiv:quant-ph/9711070) — APS. https://doi.org/10.1103/PhysRevA.60.2746 (peer-reviewed article: optimal for any success probability; parallel search gains no more than splitting the space)
  6. Common Vulnerability Scoring System v3.1: Specification Document — FIRST. https://www.first.org/cvss/v3.1/specification-document (final)
  7. D. J. Bernstein — Cost analysis of hash collisions: Will quantum computers make SHARCS obsolete? (2009) — cr.yp.to. https://cr.yp.to/hash/collisioncost-20090823.pdf (paper dated 2009-08-23)
  8. D. Litinski — How to compute a 256-bit elliptic curve private key with only 50 million Toffoli gates (arXiv:2306.08585) — arXiv. https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.08585 (preprint (2023-06-14): 6000 modules × 1152 physical qubits, one key per 10 minutes under its architecture assumptions)
  9. FIPS 203 — Module-Lattice-Based Key-Encapsulation Mechanism Standard (ML-KEM) — NIST. https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/fips/203/final (final (August 2024))
  10. FIPS 204 — Module-Lattice-Based Digital Signature Standard (ML-DSA) — NIST. https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/fips/204/final (final (August 2024))
  11. FIPS 205 — Stateless Hash-Based Digital Signature Standard (SLH-DSA) — NIST. https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/fips/205/final (final (August 2024))
  12. G. Brassard, P. Høyer, A. Tapp — Quantum Algorithm for the Collision Problem (LATIN '98; arXiv:quant-ph/9705002) — Springer / arXiv. https://arxiv.org/abs/quant-ph/9705002 (peer-reviewed article: collisions of r-to-one functions in O((N/r)^{1/3}) expected evaluations)
  13. IANA — Transport Layer Security (TLS) Parameters (Supported Groups, SignatureScheme, Cipher Suites) — IANA. https://www.iana.org/assignments/tls-parameters/ (living registry (checked 2026-10-13); 'Recommended' column Y/N/D)
  14. J. Proos, C. Zalka — Shor's discrete logarithm quantum algorithm for elliptic curves (Quantum Inf. Comput. 3(4), 2003; arXiv:quant-ph/0301141) — Quantum Information & Computation. https://doi.org/10.26421/QIC3.4-3 (peer-reviewed article)
  15. L. K. Grover — A fast quantum mechanical algorithm for database search (STOC 1996) — ACM. https://doi.org/10.1145/237814.237866 (peer-reviewed article)
  16. M. Amy et al. — Estimating the cost of generic quantum pre-image attacks on SHA-2 and SHA-3 (SAC 2016; arXiv:1603.09383) — Springer / arXiv. https://arxiv.org/abs/1603.09383 (peer-reviewed article: SHA-256 about 2^153.8 surface-code cycles on 2^12.6 logical qubits; SHA3-256 about 2^146.5 cycles on 2^20)
  17. M. Cain et al. — Shor's algorithm is possible with as few as 10,000 reconfigurable atomic qubits (arXiv:2603.28627) — arXiv. https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.28627 (preprint (2026-03-30): P-256 in a few days with 26,000 physical neutral-atom qubits; RSA-2048 one to two orders of magnitude longer)
  18. M. Ekerå, J. Gärtner — A high-level comparison of state-of-the-art quantum algorithms for breaking asymmetric cryptography (arXiv:2405.14381) — arXiv. https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.14381 (preprint (2024-05-23): Regev-style algorithms show no overall advantage yet for cryptographic RSA and finite-field sizes)
  19. M. Mosca — Cybersecurity in an Era with Quantum Computers: Will We Be Ready? (IEEE Security & Privacy, 2018) — IEEE. https://doi.org/10.1109/MSP.2018.3761723 (peer-reviewed article; source of the x + y > z migration inequality)
  20. M. Roetteler, M. Naehrig, K. Svore, K. Lauter — Quantum resource estimates for computing elliptic curve discrete logarithms (ASIACRYPT 2017) — IACR / arXiv. https://arxiv.org/abs/1706.06752 (peer-reviewed article: at most 9n + 2⌈log2 n⌉ + 10 qubits and 448 n^3 log2 n + 4090 n^3 Toffoli gates)
  21. NIST IR 8547 — Transition to Post-Quantum Cryptography Standards — NIST. https://csrc.nist.gov/pubs/ir/8547/ipd (initial public draft (November 2024); proposes deprecating quantum-vulnerable public-key algorithms after 2030 and disallowing them after 2035 — dates may change in the final version)
  22. NIST — Post-Quantum Cryptography Standardization: Evaluation Criteria, Security (Call for Proposals, Section 4.A.5) — NIST. https://csrc.nist.gov/Projects/Post-Quantum-Cryptography/Post-Quantum-Cryptography-Standardization/Evaluation-Criteria/Security-(Evaluation-Criteria) (security categories: AES-128 key search 2^170/MAXDEPTH quantum gates, AES-192 2^233/MAXDEPTH, AES-256 2^298/MAXDEPTH; MAXDEPTH 2^40–2^96)
  23. OSV advisory GHSA-2883-xcg3-v3hh — OSV.dev. https://osv.dev/vulnerability/GHSA-2883-xcg3-v3hh (modified 2026-09-08T21:30:05.575808098Z)
  24. OSV advisory GHSA-2p6r-x3vv-xqm2 — OSV.dev. https://osv.dev/vulnerability/GHSA-2p6r-x3vv-xqm2 (modified 2026-09-10T03:51:04.143649116Z)
  25. OSV advisory GHSA-3288-p39f-rqpv — OSV.dev. https://osv.dev/vulnerability/GHSA-3288-p39f-rqpv (modified 2026-09-10T03:50:59.332505686Z)
  26. OSV advisory GHSA-3pv8-6f4r-ffg2 — OSV.dev. https://osv.dev/vulnerability/GHSA-3pv8-6f4r-ffg2 (modified 2026-09-10T03:51:04.454810375Z)
  27. OSV advisory GHSA-434x-w66g-qw3r — OSV.dev. https://osv.dev/vulnerability/GHSA-434x-w66g-qw3r (modified 2026-09-10T03:50:33.995841351Z)
